And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.

The funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.

I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.

Some days I just completely forget lunch, and then other days I’m standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets, just picking at things. It’s funny, I used to keep crackers for when my neighbor’s granddaughter came by—she liked the little square ones—but now I’m the one eating them, and half the time nothing even tastes right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most mornings, I’d say. I wake up and there’s this heavy, low feeling sitting on me, like the color’s gone out of the day before I’ve even gotten dressed. And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. But my eating, that’s one of the reasons I came in. It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. I just stand there eating like I’m supposed to do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. That’s when the whole change seemed to begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. It’s embarrassing, really, because it shouldn’t be that hard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. The bills… yes, a little. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.

I put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.

It’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.

Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?

Things that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.

I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. I wake up and there’s this heavy, low feeling sitting on me, like the color’s gone out of the day before I’ve even gotten dressed. And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It’s been much longer than two weeks. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. I’m not sure what counts as intense. I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.

But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. But I do need more reminders now than I used to.

It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. I just stand there eating like I’m supposed to do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. That’s when the whole change seemed to begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. It’s embarrassing, really, because it shouldn’t be that hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… yes, a little. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.\n\nI put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. It’s silly, because the mail is just sitting there, you know, with all those little envelopes—some of them look important and some are probably just advertisements—but I can’t seem to get started.\n\nA year or two ago, I don’t think I got stuck like that as much.\n\nDoctor: Because of your thinking or memory problems, have you needed someone else to take over things like paying bills or managing your medications?\n\nPatient: Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. But I do need more reminders now than I used to.\n\nBills and pills, those things can pile up in my head—like I’ll set something down near the kitchen calendar, and then I notice the teapot needs cleaning, and then suddenly the whole morning’s gone. I’ve missed some calls and appointments that I really should have kept track of.\n\nI used to handle family things carefully, you know, birthdays and little errands, and I was teaching my craft classes too. I stopped doing those. It just got harder to keep it all straight, so I put things off more now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F32.9","G31.84","F43.20","F41.9","F50.9"],"note":"","is_final":false}

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Forgetting recent conversations… I’m not sure. Sometimes I do lose the thread of what someone said, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’m actually forgetting the conversation.

Like, my neighbor was talking to me the other day about her roses—or maybe it was the gutters, she talks about both—and I remember standing there nodding, but later I couldn’t remember why I’d gone outside in the first place. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, not like someone tells me, “You already said that,” all the time.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the thing that finally made me come in is my eating has gotten all turned around. Some days I just completely forget lunch, and then other days I’m standing at the counter eating crackers and sweets, just picking at things. It’s funny, I used to keep crackers for when my neighbor’s granddaughter came by—she liked the little square ones—but now I’m the one eating them, and half the time nothing even tastes right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most mornings, I’d say. I wake up and there’s this heavy, low feeling sitting on me, like the color’s gone out of the day before I’ve even gotten dressed. And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. But my eating, that’s one of the reasons I came in. It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. I just stand there eating like I’m supposed to do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. That’s when the whole change seemed to begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. It’s embarrassing, really, because it shouldn’t be that hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… yes, a little. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.\n\nI put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. It’s silly, because the mail is just sitting there, you know, with all those little envelopes—some of them look important and some are probably just advertisements—but I can’t seem to get started.\n\nA year or two ago, I don’t think I got stuck like that as much.\n\nDoctor: Because of your thinking or memory problems, have you needed someone else to take over things like paying bills or managing your medications?\n\nPatient: Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. But I do need more reminders now than I used to.\n\nBills and pills, those things can pile up in my head—like I’ll set something down near the kitchen calendar, and then I notice the teapot needs cleaning, and then suddenly the whole morning’s gone. I’ve missed some calls and appointments that I really should have kept track of.\n\nI used to handle family things carefully, you know, birthdays and little errands, and I was teaching my craft classes too. I stopped doing those. It just got harder to keep it all straight, so I put things off more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations?\n\nPatient: Forgetting recent conversations… I’m not sure. It’s funny, I used to keep crackers for when my neighbor’s granddaughter came by—she liked the little square ones—but now I’m the one eating them, and half the time nothing even tastes right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most mornings, I’d say. I wake up and there’s this heavy, low feeling sitting on me, like the color’s gone out of the day before I’ve even gotten dressed. And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. But my eating, that’s one of the reasons I came in. It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. It’s been much longer than two weeks. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.\n\nI put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. Yes… concentrating has been harder. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like whether I should call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind gets all foggy and I end up doing neither.

It’s funny, I used to keep crackers for when my neighbor’s granddaughter came by—she liked the little square ones—but now I’m the one eating them, and half the time nothing even tastes right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… most mornings, I’d say. I wake up and there’s this heavy, low feeling sitting on me, like the color’s gone out of the day before I’ve even gotten dressed. And I know that sounds silly, but it’s like when the curtains are open and it still feels dim. I used to notice the birds out back first thing, but lately I just lie there a bit and think, “Oh… another day.”\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. But my eating, that’s one of the reasons I came in. It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. I just stand there eating like I’m supposed to do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. That’s when the whole change seemed to begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. It’s embarrassing, really, because it shouldn’t be that hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… yes, a little. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.\n\nI put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. It’s silly, because the mail is just sitting there, you know, with all those little envelopes—some of them look important and some are probably just advertisements—but I can’t seem to get started.\n\nA year or two ago, I don’t think I got stuck like that as much.\n\nDoctor: Because of your thinking or memory problems, have you needed someone else to take over things like paying bills or managing your medications?\n\nPatient: Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. But I do need more reminders now than I used to.\n\nBills and pills, those things can pile up in my head—like I’ll set something down near the kitchen calendar, and then I notice the teapot needs cleaning, and then suddenly the whole morning’s gone. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. It’s silly, because the mail is just sitting there, you know, with all those little envelopes—some of them look important and some are probably just advertisements—but I can’t seem to get started.\n\nA year or two ago, I don’t think I got stuck like that as much.\n\nDoctor: Because of your thinking or memory problems, have you needed someone else to take over things like paying bills or managing your medications?\n\nPatient: Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. I’ve missed some calls and appointments that I really should have kept track of.\n\nI used to handle family things carefully, you know, birthdays and little errands, and I was teaching my craft classes too. I stopped doing those. It just got harder to keep it all straight, so I put things off more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations?\n\nPatient: Forgetting recent conversations… I’m not sure. Sometimes I do lose the thread of what someone said, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’m actually forgetting the conversation.\n\nLike, my neighbor was talking to me the other day about her roses—or maybe it was the gutters, she talks about both—and I remember standing there nodding, but later I couldn’t remember why I’d gone outside in the first place. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, not like someone tells me, “You already said that,” all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I used to really look forward to my watercolor class, and the quilting circle too—those ladies always bring too many cookies, and someone’s always fussing over the binding on a quilt. And book club, I used to keep the library receipt tucked in the book so I wouldn’t lose my place… but lately I just leave my bag sitting by the door. I see it there, ready to go, and I just… can’t make myself care enough to pick it up and leave.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes… not like I’ve made a plan or anything like that. I haven’t. It’s more… I’ll be lying there in the morning, and I think, well, if I just didn’t wake up, maybe that would be easier. Maybe a relief to everyone.\n\nAnd then I feel awful for even thinking it, because my sister used to say you should never talk that way, and she had this little plaque in her kitchen—oh, I can still picture it—but… yes. Those thoughts do come.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: A little… well, I don’t know if it’s memory exactly. It’s more like my mind gets all cloudy. I’ll sit there looking at the mail on the table, and I’ll think, “Should I open that first, or should I call the pharmacy?” And then I just keep staring at it.\n\nThe funny thing is, I used to be so particular about mail—my husband always said I could spot a bill through the envelope—but now I’ll sit there so long thinking about what to do first that I end up doing neither. It makes me feel… dull, I guess. Foggy.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost weight recently without trying to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure about weight exactly… I haven’t been checking the scale much. But my eating, that’s one of the reasons I came in. It’s all gotten turned around.\n\nSome days I just forget lunch altogether, and then other days I’ll be standing at the counter eating crackers or something sweet, just picking at it, even though it doesn’t taste right. I bought those little sandwich cookies because they were on sale—my neighbor used to put them out with tea—but I don’t even enjoy them. I just stand there eating like I’m supposed to do something with my hands.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life around the time these symptoms began?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. It really started when my sister’s health took that serious turn. That was—let me think—about a year and five months ago now.\n\nFunny how I count it by that, not by the calendar. She always kept the calendar on the fridge with everyone’s birthdays written in red pen, and I never did it the same way after… but yes, around then. That’s when the whole change seemed to begin.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… nearly every day, I’d say. It’s been much longer than two weeks. That low, colorless feeling—like somebody took the brightness out of things—has been with me about a year and two months now.\n\nI remember because it sort of runs together with the seasons… I used to notice when the light changed in the afternoon, but now it all feels the same. So yes, it’s been there most days for quite a while.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself forgetting recent conversations or appointments?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s forgetting exactly… appointments, I write those little notes, though sometimes the paper ends up under the grocery ads and then, well, that’s another story.\n\nBut I do notice my mind gets foggy. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail first, and I can’t seem to choose. Then I end up doing neither. It’s embarrassing, really, because it shouldn’t be that hard.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble managing your bills?\n\nPatient: The bills… yes, a little. I mean, I still manage to stay in my house and keep things from falling completely apart, but I don’t do it the way I used to.\n\nI put things off now. I’ll see an envelope on the table and think, “I should open that,” and then somehow I’m wiping the counter or looking for a pen, and the whole afternoon goes. I use more notes and reminders than I used to.\n\nIt’s not just bills, really. I’ve missed calls and appointments, and family things I always used to handle carefully. I even stopped teaching my craft classes… and I loved getting the supplies sorted, the little ribbons and beads. But bills, yes, they’ve become harder to stay on top of.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself forgetting things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Recently… I’m not sure. I mean, sometimes I’ll walk into the kitchen and forget why I went in there, but people say that happens at my age, don’t they?\n\nThings that happened, like conversations or… what I had for lunch yesterday? I don’t know. The days kind of fold into each other. I can remember the old things better sometimes, like my mother’s sewing tin—funny, it had this little blue lid, and I used to sort buttons in it for hours.\n\nBut recent things… I haven’t really noticed losing whole events, no. Or maybe I’m not paying close enough attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself intensely longing for your sister?\n\nPatient: My sister… well, I think about her sometimes, sure. We had this old set of dishes she liked, little flowers around the edge—funny how something like that sticks in your head.\n\nBut intensely longing? I don’t know if I’d put it that way. I’m not sure what counts as intense. Sometimes people come to mind, especially when the house is quiet, but I haven’t really noticed it as something overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually nervous or worried a lot?\n\nPatient: Nervous or worried a lot… I don’t know. I suppose I worry some, like anyone does. There’s always something in a house making a noise, or a letter that needs sorting, and then I remember I meant to buy more stamps—do people still use stamps much? My niece said everything’s online now.\n\nBut unusually nervous… I haven’t really noticed that, no. Not like I’m sitting there shaking or anything. I guess I’d have to think about what “a lot” means.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your thinking or memory has clearly gotten worse compared with how it was a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe, yes. Not memory like forgetting my whole life or anything, but my thinking feels slower than it used to.\n\nSometimes I’ll sit there with something simple in front of me, like, should I call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind just gets foggy. I end up staring at both and doing neither. It’s silly, because the mail is just sitting there, you know, with all those little envelopes—some of them look important and some are probably just advertisements—but I can’t seem to get started.\n\nA year or two ago, I don’t think I got stuck like that as much.\n\nDoctor: Because of your thinking or memory problems, have you needed someone else to take over things like paying bills or managing your medications?\n\nPatient: Not completely take over, no… I mean, I still live at home and I’m mostly managing. But I do need more reminders now than I used to.\n\nBills and pills, those things can pile up in my head—like I’ll set something down near the kitchen calendar, and then I notice the teapot needs cleaning, and then suddenly the whole morning’s gone. I’ve missed some calls and appointments that I really should have kept track of.\n\nI used to handle family things carefully, you know, birthdays and little errands, and I was teaching my craft classes too. I stopped doing those. It just got harder to keep it all straight, so I put things off more now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been forgetting recent conversations?\n\nPatient: Forgetting recent conversations… I’m not sure. Sometimes I do lose the thread of what someone said, especially if there’s a lot going on, but I don’t know if I’m actually forgetting the conversation.\n\nLike, my neighbor was talking to me the other day about her roses—or maybe it was the gutters, she talks about both—and I remember standing there nodding, but later I couldn’t remember why I’d gone outside in the first place. So… I don’t know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, not like someone tells me, “You already said that,” all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I know my usual places—the grocery store, my street, the little post office with the funny parking lot. Sometimes I’ll pause and think, now why did I come down this aisle, but that’s different, isn’t it?\n\nI did once go the long way around because they moved the bread display, and then I got annoyed because stores are always moving things around like it’s a puzzle. But getting lost in a place I know… no, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yes… concentrating has been harder. I’ll sit there staring at something simple, like whether I should call the pharmacy first or open the mail, and then my mind gets all foggy and I end up doing neither.\n\nIt’s silly, because it’s not like those are complicated things. The mail is right there, usually by the chair, and then I start thinking I should find my glasses, and then I notice a coupon from the grocery store… anyway, yes, I do have trouble keeping my mind on one thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been moving or talking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My body feels kind of slowed down lately, like everything takes longer than it should. Crossing the room, buttoning my sweater—little things like that feel like I’m moving through thick air or something.\n\nBut then other times I’ll find myself pacing around the house, holding the same papers in my hand, like I’m supposed to be doing something with them. I don’t even get anywhere with it, just back and forth. So it’s both, I guess… slow, but restless too.\n\nDoctor: Have you had less appetite than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… sort of. My eating’s been all turned around, and that’s actually one of the things that made me come in. Some days I’ll forget lunch completely, just plain forget, and then I’ll notice it’s late and I’ve only had tea or maybe toast.\n\nBut then other days I’m standing at the counter eating crackers, or those little sweets I keep meaning not to buy, even though they don’t even taste right. I don’t know if that counts as less appetite exactly… it’s just not normal for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
